--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 for every **stationary** robot → robots should **move** before switch
- After switch time: score += 1 for every **moving** robot → robots should **stop** after switch
- Switch signal: a wall block lights up **GREEN** at switch time
- Floor is uniformly GRAY throughout

**Optimal strategy:**
1. **Before switch**: all robots move (exploration/random walk)
2. **After switch**: all robots stop when they see GREEN

**Key perception**: The green light on the wall is visible via camera (C=7, colour=2 for green). When a robot perceives green, it should switch to Stop.

**State design:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when green is perceived
- State 1: Stop → maybe transition back if needed, or stay

The tricky part is that once stopped, they might miss the green signal if they're already stopped. Also, robots that stop early (before switch) are penalized. So we want robots moving until they see green, then stopping.

Let me design multiple machines with variations:

**Base design (2 states):**
- S0: Exploration (moving) → go to S1 when sees green (C=7, l=2)
- S1: Stop → stay (or small prob transition back)

**Variations to explore:**
- Different rwm values for exploration
- Different probabilities for green detection
- GoToColor green (approach the green wall) then stop
- 3-state machines with attraction/repulsion
- Adding neighbor-based transitions

Let me create 14 machines:

1. **Elitism/best guess**: S0=Exploration, S1=Stop; transition on green
2-7. Small variations of the base
8-14. Alternative topologies

Since Round 1 just completed but no scores were given (it says "Round 1 completed"), I'll design from scratch based on mission analysis.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I still don't have explicit scores from Round 2, but I can reason about the mission:

**Core insight:**
- Before switch (~400-600 steps): robots should MOVE → Exploration is good
- After switch: robots should STOP → Stop behavior needed
- Signal: GREEN wall appears at switch time → C=7 with l=2 (green) is the key trigger

**The fundamental challenge**: 
- Robots that see green should stop
- But not all robots may see green immediately (only those facing the wall)
- Need a way to propagate the "stop" signal to robots that don't see green directly

**Propagation idea**: If robots that see green stop, others can detect "many neighbors stopped" (low movement patterns) but ARGoS doesn't directly sense neighbor motion. However, if stopped robots are near the green wall, we could use neighbor count (C=3/C=4) as indirect signal.

**Better propagation**: If robots seeing green emit a specific LED color, others can detect it via C=7. Let's try:
- Moving robots: LED off or yellow
- Stopped robots: LED green (color 2... wait, LED colors are {0,4,5,6} = off, yellow, magenta, cyan)

LED colors available: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. No green LED available! So we can't use LED signaling for green.

**Alternative propagation via neighbor count**: When some robots stop near the green wall, others might detect high neighbor counts (robots clustering near wall) — but this is indirect.

**Simplest effective design**: 2-state machine where Exploration → Stop on seeing green (p=0.9-0.99). The probability should be high so robots stop quickly upon seeing green. The Stop state should have very low probability of returning to Exploration.

**Key refinement**: The probability parameter for C=7 acts as a per-step firing probability when the condition is true. So p=0.9 means 90% chance per step of transitioning when green is visible. This should work well.

Let me focus my 14 machines on:
1. Best guess (elitism): 2-state Explore→Stop on green, p=0.95, low return prob
2. Variations on this topology
3. Some 3-state machines for propagation

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I'm now in Round 4 without explicit score feedback, but I need to think more carefully about what's actually happening.

**Re-examining the mission:**
- Before switch (400-600 steps): score += 1 per stationary robot → robots must MOVE
- After switch: score += 1 per moving robot → robots must STOP
- Green wall appears at switch time

**Score estimation for baseline strategies:**
- Pure Exploration (always moving): ~600 steps × 20 robots = 12000 post-switch penalty (600 worst case)
- Pure Stop (always still): ~500 steps × 20 robots = 10000 pre-switch penalty (500 avg)
- Optimal: 0 pre-switch (all moving) + 0 post-switch (all stopped) = 0... but transition takes time

**Critical issue I may have missed**: The green light is on a WALL, not the floor. Robots use camera to detect colored objects. The C=7 condition checks if the robot perceives a given colour with its camera. This should work for wall-mounted green lights.

**Another critical issue**: Not all robots will face the green wall simultaneously. Robots facing away won't see it. They need to turn to see it. With exploration (random walk), they'll eventually face it.

**The attraction idea**: GoToColor green (B=8) might actually help - robots would actively seek the green wall, see it, then... but B=8 keeps them moving toward it. We need them to STOP after seeing it.

**Propagation problem**: Robots far from the green wall or facing away need another signal. Options:
1. Random small probability of stopping (catches some robots by chance)
2. Neighbor count threshold (if many neighbors are stopped/near wall)

**What if the best strategy is simpler?** A robot that moves before the switch and stops after needs to detect the switch. Green is the ONLY signal. So the architecture must be:
- State: Move → on green detected → State: Stop

But the key is: robots NOT seeing green continue moving (accumulating score). We need them to stop too.

**Propagation via LED**: Stopped robots could emit cyan (6), moving robots emit nothing. Other robots detect cyan via C=7 l=6... wait, cyan is LED color 6, but C=7 looks for colors {1,2,3,4,5,6}. So cyan LED (6) CAN be detected! This is a relay mechanism!

**Relay design**:
- S0: Exploration, LED off → if sees green (wall, color 2) → S1
- S1: Stop, LED cyan (6) → stays (very low prob return)
- Other robots: if they see cyan (another stopped robot's LED) → also stop

But we need a 3-state machine:
- S0: Exploration, LED 0 → sees green (C=7, l=2) → S1; or sees cyan (C=7, l=6) → S1
- S1: Stop, LED 6 (cyan) → tiny prob → S0

Actually with 2 transitions from S0 both going to S1, that's valid!

Let me design this relay approach carefully:

FSM designs:
1. **Best relay 3-state**: Move→Stop(green), Move→Stop(cyan from others), Stop emits cyan
2. **Simple 2-state variants** (elitism of previous best guess)
3. **Various explorations**

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.95 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 7 --l0x1 6 --p0x1 0.95 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.99 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 7 --l0x1 6 --p0x1 0.99 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 20 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.95 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 7 --l0x1 6 --p0x1 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I've been running for 4 rounds without score feedback. Let me think more carefully about what might actually be working and what might not.

**Key reconsideration:**

The LED relay idea (stop emits cyan, others detect cyan) is theoretically sound, but I need to verify my understanding of C=7: it detects colored objects visible to the camera. Wall blocks and robot LEDs are both colored objects. So robots can detect:
- Green wall (color 2) via C=7 l=2
- Cyan LED (color 6) on other robots via C=7 l=6
- Magenta LED (color 5) on other robots via C=7 l=5

**What could be going wrong?**

1. The range-and-bearing is 0.5m, but camera range might be different (potentially longer for colored objects)
2. The green wall might use a different color index than 2
3. The relay might work but the probabilities need tuning

**Color mapping concern**: The problem says green wall. Color options are {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Standard ARGoS colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So green=2 should be correct.

**Deeper issue**: Maybe the green detection isn't working as expected, or the timing is off. Let me think about scores:

- If all robots always move: penalty = (600 steps after switch) × 20 robots = 12000 max
- If all robots always stop: penalty = (500 steps before switch avg) × 20 robots = 10000 avg
- Pure Stop might actually score ~10000, Pure Explore ~12000

Wait - **pure Stop scores less than pure Explore**! That's an important insight. So if our detect-green machines aren't working well, a bias toward stopping might actually be better.

**Better baseline**: What if we bias toward stopping with occasional movement? Or what about the **timing**: if switch happens at t~500, and run is 1200 steps, then:
- Pre-switch: ~500 steps, stationary penalty
- Post-switch: ~700 steps, moving penalty

So post-switch penalty period is LONGER than pre-switch. This means stopping early (even before seeing green) isn't terrible - you pay ~500 steps of pre-switch penalty but save ~700 steps of post-switch penalty... but only if you stop at the RIGHT time.

**The fundamental tension**: If you stop too early, you pay pre-switch penalty. If you stay moving too long post-switch, you pay more. The green signal is the ONLY reliable indicator.

**What about a timed approach?** There's no timer in AutoMoDe, but you could use a random probability to transition: if p=1/500, after ~500 steps you'd transition. But this is noisy.

**Refined strategy**: 
- Move with exploration (low rwm for smooth movement to cover more area and see green faster)
- Transition to Stop on green (high probability)
- Stopped robots emit cyan LED to relay signal
- Other moving robots also stop on seeing cyan

**Trying different color for relay**: Use magenta (5) instead of cyan (6) - same logic, just different color to compare.

**Also trying**: GoToColor green (B=8) as a way to orient toward green wall, making detection more likely, then stopping.

Let me produce 14 machines with emphasis on the relay mechanism with varied parameters:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 5 rounds without score feedback, I need to fundamentally reconsider my approach. Let me think more carefully.

**What I've been submitting**: Mostly 2-state Explore→Stop machines triggered by green detection (C=7 l=2) and cyan relay (C=7 l=6). These are theoretically sound.

**Why they might not be scoring well:**

1. **Green color index**: The problem says the wall "lights up GREEN". In ARGoS, colored objects detected by camera might use different color indices than I assume. Color 2 might not be green in this context. Let me reconsider: {1,2,3,4,5,6} - the mapping isn't explicitly given. It could be 1=green instead of 2=green.

2. **Camera vs RAB**: The C=7 condition uses the camera to detect colored objects. Wall blocks that light up might be visible. But I'm not 100% sure the color index 2 = green.

3. **The relay via LED might not work if LED range is very short** - robots need to be within visual range.

**Let me try color 1 as green** as an alternative hypothesis.

**Also reconsidering**: Maybe the best strategy doesn't rely on green detection at all. Let me think about what scores are achievable:

- Always moving: penalty = post-switch steps × 20 ≈ 700 × 20 = 14000 (worst case 800 × 20 = 16000)
- Always stopped: penalty = pre-switch steps × 20 ≈ 500 × 20 = 10000
- **Best possible**: 0 (all move pre-switch, all stop post-switch)

If green detection works perfectly, we'd approach 0. But if it doesn't work at all (wrong color index), we're just doing pure Exploration which scores ~14000.

**Try both color 1 and color 2** for green detection.

**Another angle**: What if I use a probabilistic stop approach? If robots randomly stop with some probability that increases over time... but AutoMoDe doesn't have timers. However, C=5 (fixed probability per step) could be used to create a drift toward stopping.

**Interesting design**: Start in Explore, transition to Stop with tiny probability p per step. Expected time to stop = 1/p steps. If p = 1/500, average transition at step 500. But variance is huge. Some robots stop at step 100, others at 1000.

**What if we use neighbor count as proxy?** After switch, green wall appears. Robots near the wall stop (detect green). Now there's a cluster of stopped robots near the green wall. Other robots could detect "many neighbors" and stop too. But this requires robots to be near each other.

**Mixed approach**: Try color 1 AND color 2 for green, keep relay mechanism, also try probabilistic timed approach.

Let me distribute my 14 machines:
- 3 machines with color=1 for green detection (new hypothesis)
- 3 machines with color=2 (current hypothesis, relay)  
- 2 machines with color=1 AND relay via cyan
- 2 machines with probabilistic timed stopping
- 2 machines with GoToColor (color 1 or 2) then stop
- 2 exploration machines for coverage

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 6 rounds with no score feedback provided to me, I need to think even more carefully. The budget is decreasing (568 left from original ~600/round × 10 rounds), which is normal.

**Fundamental reassessment:**

Let me reconsider what colors map to what indices. The problem states colors {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Looking at ARGoS/AutoMoDe documentation more carefully:
- The colored wall block is GREEN
- LED colors available: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan (the problem explicitly states these)
- For C=7 (camera detection): {1,2,3,4,5,6}

The fact that LED colors are {0,4,5,6} (not including 1,2,3) suggests the camera color indices {1,2,3,4,5,6} don't directly map to the same scheme. OR: the camera detects both wall colors AND LED colors, and the numbering is:
- 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

And LEDs can only be 0(off), 4(yellow), 5(magenta), 6(cyan) because those are the only available LED colors for e-puck in AutoMoDe.

So green wall = color 2 for camera detection. My original assumption was correct.

**Why might the machines be performing poorly?**

Maybe the issue is that I don't have score feedback and I'm actually doing well! Or maybe the green wall detection genuinely doesn't work because:
1. The camera might not detect wall colors in this ARGoS setup
2. The range might be insufficient

**Critical insight I've been missing**: What if the camera in AutoMoDe's e-puck only detects OTHER ROBOTS' LEDs, not wall blocks? In some ARGoS configurations, the colored blob camera only sees robot LEDs, not arena objects. If that's the case, then C=7 l=2 (green wall) NEVER fires!

If the camera only detects robot LEDs, then:
- The green wall signal is INVISIBLE to robots
- The only way to detect switch is... there is no direct way!
- Best strategy becomes: move before likely switch time, then stop

**Without any detection mechanism**, we need a timed strategy:
- Move for ~500 steps (pre-switch period), then stop
- Use C=5 (probabilistic) with p ≈ 1/500 to create gradual transition

But with p=0.002 per step, expected transition at step 500. Half the robots would transition by step ~350 (too early), variance is huge.

**Alternative**: What if floor color changes? Problem says "floor is uniformly GRAY" - no floor color change.

**What if range-and-bearing (neighbor count) changes?** If some robots stop near the green wall... the neighbor count changes but that's very indirect.

**Maybe the best we can do without detecting the green wall** is to try to minimize BOTH penalties:
- A robot that moves the ENTIRE run: ~700 steps × 20 = 14000 post-switch penalty, 0 pre-switch
- A robot that stops the ENTIRE run: ~500 steps × 20 = 10000 pre-switch, 0 post-switch
- **Stopping wins if green can't be detected!**

Wait, let me recalculate. If switch is uniform in [400,600]:
- Always moving: E[post-switch penalty] = E[1200 - T] × 20 = (1200 - 500) × 20 = 14000
- Always stopping: E[pre-switch penalty] = E[T] × 20 = 500 × 20 = 10000

So **pure Stop** scores ~10000 on average, better than pure Explore (~14000).

**But optimal is near 0** if we can detect the switch.

**New hypothesis**: Maybe I should try ALL color indices (1-6) for the green wall more systematically, AND try the relay more carefully.

**Also**: What if the best approach is to use a neighbor-count based relay? When robots near the green wall stop, others moving toward them see increased neighbor counts. If exploring robots stop when they see many neighbors...

Actually let me try a completely different approach: **Start in Stop, transition to Explore with small probability, then back to Stop on green/cyan**. Wait, that's backwards - we want to MOVE before switch.

**Or: Start in Explore, randomly stop after ~400-600 steps using geometric distribution approximation.**

With p_stop = 0.002 per step: E[stop time] = 500 steps. This is actually a reasonable approximation!

**Best design without green detection:**
- State 0: Explore (rwm=10) → Stop with p=0.002 per step
- State 1: Stop → stay (very low return prob)

This gives an expected transition at step 500, which aligns with the average switch time!

Let me compute the expected score for this strategy:
- Robot stops at step T_stop ~ Geometric(0.002)
- Switch at T_switch ~ Uniform(400,600)
- Pre-switch penalty if T_stop < T_switch: (T_switch - T_stop) steps stationary
- Post-switch penalty if T_stop > T_switch: (T_stop - T_switch) steps moving + (1200 - T_stop) × ... wait, robot stops at T_stop and stays stopped

Actually this is complex. The point is: if T_stop ≈ T_switch, we minimize both penalties. The geometric distribution with p=0.002 centered at 500 is a reasonable bet.

**My new population:**
1. Probabilistic timed stop: p=0.002 (E=500 steps)
2. Probabilistic timed stop: p=0.0015 (E=667 steps, biased later)
3. Probabilistic timed stop: p=0.0025 (E=400 steps, biased earlier)
4-6. Green detection (color 2) + relay (keep trying)
7-9. Green detection (color 1) + relay
10-11. Both color 1 and 2 + relay
12-13. GoToColor then stop

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 7 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to make my best judgment. Let me think very carefully about what's likely working.

**Core analysis of the timed approach:**

With p=0.002 per step in Exploration → Stop:
- Expected transition: step 500 (center of switch window [400,600])
- But robots transition independently with high variance (geometric distribution, std dev ≈ 500)
- Many robots will stop at step 100, others at step 1000
- Pre-switch penalty for early stoppers, post-switch penalty for late ones

**Can we do better than random timing?**

The key insight: ALL robots start at step 0 and the switch happens at step ~500. If we could coordinate stopping at exactly step 500, we'd minimize penalty. But without a timer, we can't.

**What if we use a two-phase approach?**
- Phase 1 (Explore, rwm=10): robots move around
- After ~N steps of exploration, probability of stopping increases

But AutoMoDe has no memory of time elapsed.

**The neighbor-count idea revisited:**
After switch, green wall lights up. Robots near the green wall stop (if they detect it). This creates a cluster of stopped robots. Other robots could detect this cluster via C=3 (neighbors above threshold) or C=4 (neighbors below threshold). But this requires robots to be near each other.

Actually wait - if robots that stop near the green wall emit cyan LED, and then nearby robots see cyan and also stop, those new stopped robots also emit cyan... This creates a propagation wave! The key is that robots need to be within camera range (~0.5m? or maybe longer for camera).

**Reconsideration of the problem**: The range-and-bearing is 0.5m for neighbor counting, but the camera range for colored objects might be much longer (typically 0.7m or even longer in ARGoS). Wall blocks are large colored objects visible from afar.

**Let me try a completely different approach**: What if I use neighbor count to detect the "stopped cluster"?

After switch, robots near green wall stop. If they cluster near the wall (stopped), robots approaching see many neighbors. So: Explore → if neighbors > threshold → Stop.

But this could also trigger during normal exploration if robots happen to cluster.

**Hybrid strategy**: Explore → (green detected OR cyan detected OR p_random) → Stop

The p_random fallback ensures eventual stopping even without detecting green.

**Let me also reconsider timing**: 
- Run = 1200 steps
- Switch at T ~ U[400,600], mean 500
- Post-switch period: 1200-T ~ U[600,800], mean 700
- Pre-switch period: T ~ U[400,600], mean 500

So the post-switch penalty per moving robot (700 steps) > pre-switch penalty per stopped robot (500 steps). This means it's slightly better to stop too early than too late!

Optimal p for geometric stop: should be slightly higher than 1/500, maybe 1/450 or so, to bias toward stopping a bit earlier.

**Also**: Multiple independent transitions from Explore to Stop (green + cyan + random) all fire independently, which is good - first one to fire wins. So having all three is better than any one alone.

**My best design:**
```
State 0: Explore (rwm=10), LED off
  → Stop if sees green (color 2), p=0.99
  → Stop if sees cyan (color 6, relay), p=0.99  
  → Stop randomly, p=0.002

State 1: Stop, LED cyan (6)
  → Explore with tiny p=0.0005 (almost never return)
```

**For the 13 machines**, I'll focus on:
1-3: The best hybrid design with variations on rwm and probabilities
4-5: Pure timed approach (p=0.002, p=0.0018)
6-8: Green detection (color 2) + relay + random, different rwm
9-10: Color 1 + relay + random (hedge on color index)
11-12: 3-state with attraction intermediate state
13: 4-transition exploration state

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I'm now in Round 9 with no explicit score feedback still. Let me think very carefully about what's most likely to work.

**Fundamental reconsideration of the mission:**

The scoring:
- Before switch T (uniform [400,600]): +1 per stationary robot per step
- After switch T: +1 per moving robot per step

**Expected scores for simple strategies:**
- Always move: E[score] = E[(1200-T)] × 20 = 700 × 20 = 14,000
- Always stop: E[score] = E[T] × 20 = 500 × 20 = 10,000
- Perfect switch: E[score] ≈ 0

**The timed stop (p=0.002):**
Each robot stops independently at geometric(0.002) ~ mean 500.
- If robot stops at step S, switch at T:
  - If S < T: pre-switch penalty = T - S steps stationary
  - If S > T: post-switch penalty = S - T steps moving (then 0 after stopping)
  - Plus: after stopping, 0 penalty regardless

Expected penalty per robot ≈ E[|S - T|] where S~Geometric(0.002), T~Uniform[400,600]

This is much better than always-stop (10,000) or always-move (14,000).

**Can I do better?** The key question is whether green detection works.

**Let me think about what "lights up GREEN" means in ARGoS**: The wall block changes color. In ARGoS, colored boxes/cylinders can be detected by the colored blob camera. The color index for green in ARGoS is typically 2 (RGB: green). So C=7 l=2 should detect it.

**Why might green detection not work?**
- The camera might be omnidirectional OR directional - if directional, robot must face the wall
- The wall might be far away and camera range limited
- Maybe the green block is small and hard to detect reliably

**Key issue with directional camera**: If robots are exploring with rwm=10 (slow turning), they might not face the green wall often enough. Higher rwm = more turning = more likely to face green wall eventually.

**Another key issue**: After the switch, robots that are exploring will eventually face the green wall. With rwm=10, the robot turns at a moderate rate. The question is how quickly they detect it.

**Let me estimate**: If the camera has a 240° field of view (typical for e-puck blob camera), and the green wall is one of 4 walls, the probability of facing it at any moment is ~240/360 × 1/4 ≈ 17% if walls are equally visible. So detection should happen within a few steps.

**But the real issue**: After switch, if green detection works with p=0.99, robots should stop within 1-2 steps of seeing green. The question is how many steps until they see it. With random walk, probably 10-30 steps on average. So the expected delay is small.

**Why might my machines not be performing better than baseline?**

Possibility 1: The machines ARE working well and I just don't have score feedback.
Possibility 2: There's a bug in my FSM syntax.
Possibility 3: Green detection doesn't work (wrong color index or camera limitation).

**Let me try a completely different set of color indices**: Try colors 3, 4, 5 for the green wall - maybe the mapping is non-standard.

Actually, let me reconsider. The problem says "one wall block lights up GREEN." In ARGoS, if a box lights up green, its color code would be green. The AutoMoDe documentation says C=7 detects "a given colour." The colors {1,2,3,4,5,6} - without explicit mapping, let me try all remaining options.

Colors I've tried: 1, 2, 6 (as relay)
Colors not tried as main: 3, 4, 5

**But wait**: Colors 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan match LED colors. Colors 1,2,3 might be red, green, blue. Green=2 seems most natural.

**New strategy: Exhaustive color search combined with timed fallback:**
Try detecting colors 1,2,3,4,5 for the green wall, with a timed fallback (p=0.002) as insurance.

**Also**: Let me reconsider the LED relay colors. If stopped robots emit cyan (6), and the camera detects cyan (6)... but wait, do robot LEDs appear as colored blobs to the camera? In some ARGoS setups, LEDs are only detectable via range-and-bearing, not camera. If that's the case, the relay doesn't work via C=7.

**Most robust design**: 
- State 0: Explore, try ALL detectable colors + timed fallback
- State 1: Stop, low return probability

With 4 transitions max:
- C=7 l=1 (red?), p=0.99
- C=7 l=2 (green?), p=0.99  
- C=7 l=3 (blue?), p=0.99
- C=5 p=0.002 (timed fallback)

This covers maximum color possibilities while maintaining timing fallback.

**For 13 machines:**
1. Best hybrid: 4 transitions (colors 1,2,3 + timed)
2. Colors 1,2,6 + timed
3. Colors 2,3,4 + timed  
4. Colors 1,2 + timed (3 transitions)
5. Colors 2,3 + timed (3 transitions)
6. Pure color 2 + timed (2 transitions)
7. Pure color 1 + timed (2 transitions)
8. Pure timed p=0.002
9. Pure timed p=0.0018 (bias earlier)
10. 3-state: explore → GoToColor2 → stop
11. 3-state with relay
12. Color 2 + relay + timed (3 transitions)
13. Colors 1,2,3,4 (all 4 slots)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. I need to make my best possible 12 machines.

**What I know after 9 rounds:**
- I've been submitting green-detection + timed-stop machines consistently
- No score feedback has been given to me, so I can't tell what's working
- I need to cover my bases with the most robust designs

**Final analysis of the mission:**

The score is minimized. Best strategy:
- Move before switch (penalty for stationary)
- Stop after switch (penalty for moving)
- Switch signaled by GREEN wall

**What I believe is most likely working:**
The timed approach (Explore → Stop with p≈0.002) gives expected stop time at step 500, matching the average switch time. This is a solid baseline.

Green detection (C=7 l=2) should work if the camera can see wall objects. The relay (cyan LED) should propagate the stop signal.

**Final design priorities:**

1. **Best guess (elitism)**: The 4-transition machine covering colors 1,2,3 + timed (p=0.002) has the broadest coverage
2. **Exploit**: Variations on the 3-transition machines (color 2 + cyan relay + timed)
3. **Explore**: Try some different timings and structures

**Critical optimization for final round**: 

Since post-switch penalty period (mean 700 steps) > pre-switch penalty period (mean 500 steps), stopping slightly early is better than stopping late. This suggests p slightly higher than 0.002.

Optimal p calculation: minimize E[penalty per robot]
- E[penalty] = E[max(0, T-S)×1_stationary + max(0, S-T)×1_moving_post_switch]
  where S = stop time, T = switch time
  
Actually: if S > T: robot is moving during [T, S] → penalty = S - T
If S < T: robot is stopped during [S, T] → penalty = T - S
E[penalty] = E[|S - T|]

This is minimized when S = T in expectation. But since T~U[400,600] (mean 500) and asymmetry of the run (more time post-switch), we want E[S] ≈ 500.

With p=0.002, E[S] = 500. With p=0.0025, E[S] = 400. With p=0.0017, E[S] = 588.

The penalty is symmetric in |S-T|, but there's an additional consideration: after step 1200, the run ends. If S > 1200, robot never stops → full post-switch penalty. So we don't want p too low.

**Also**: If green detection works, robots stop much faster after switch (within ~10-20 steps of seeing green), making the timed fallback mostly irrelevant. The combination is best.

**For 12 machines, my final population:**

1. Color 1+2+3 + timed (p=0.002) - broadest color coverage
2. Color 1+2+6 + timed (p=0.002) - with cyan relay
3. Color 2+6 + timed (p=0.002) - focused
4. Color 1+2+3 + timed (p=0.002), rwm=20 - more turning
5. Color 2 + timed (p=0.002) - simple
6. Pure timed p=0.002 - fallback
7. Pure timed p=0.0018 - slightly later stop
8. Color 1+2+3+6 all 4 transitions - maximum coverage
9. 3-state with color detection + relay propagation
10. Color 2+3+6 + timed
11. 3-state with GoToColor then stop
12. Color 1+2 + timed, different rwm

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
